EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
INTRODUCTION 
The California Governor’s Office of Emergency Services (Cal OES), Office of 
Grants Management, Victim Services (VS) Branch presents the annual fiscal and 
programmatic status report to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee for fiscal 
year (FY) 2022-23. This report provides the Legislature, our stakeholders, and 
Subrecipients with a comprehensive understanding of the work being 
accomplished across California through our programs. This report lists each of 
the VS Branch’s programs, Grant Subawards, fund sources, funded amounts, 
budget program/components, and the resulting statistics gathered for 
FY 2021-22 (July 1, 2022, to June 30, 2023) for each VS Branch program. 
In FY 2022-23, the VS Branch administered approximately $374 million in funding, 
through programs that respond to victims’ needs or promote an effective 
criminal justice system response to victims. The following pages provide a 
snapshot of the activities, allocations, and statistical data related to the 77 
victim services grant programs administered by the VS Branch. The below table 
is a sampling of the total data collected:

PROGRAM HIGHLIGHTS 
The Program Highlights that are included below showcase the outstanding 
efforts of state, local, and non-profit victim services and criminal justice 
organizations and programs that Cal OES’s VS Branch funds. The funding allows 
for prevention, intervention, and enforcement components that work toward 
achieving a safer California. The need for these services is on-going – without 
continued state and federal grant funding, many of these exemplary projects 
would cease to exist. 
AMERICAN RESCUE PLAN UNIT 
Venice Family Clinic (VFC), a Subrecipient of the Domestic Violence American 
Rescue Plan Program, was founded in 1970 and has grown into a Federally 
Qualified Health Center with 10 clinical sites in and around the Westside of Los 
Angeles, CA. With a mission to provide quality primary health care to people in 
need, VFC offers an array of comprehensive services to almost 27,000 people 
1 



--- PAGE 5 ---
 
 
 
   
 
   
 
 
     
 
   
  
 
 
 
  
  
  
 
 
 
    
  
     
 
  
 
  
   
    
 
   
   
  
  
   
 
  
annually, including the Safe Families Domestic Violence Intervention and 
Prevention Program. 
Though perhaps not a typical setting for a domestic violence intervention 
program, VFC understands, that as a community health center (Clinic) they 
have a unique opportunity to address the issue. The survivor’s visits can appear 
simply as a primary care medical visit, but in reality, the certified Domestic 
Violence Specialist provides individual counseling in Spanish or English to help 
survivors understand the dynamics of violence, assess their needs, develop 
safety plans, and examine the impact of violence on children and other family 
members. 
The emergence of the COVID-19 pandemic trapped survivors of violence in 
close confines with their abusers, leaving them at increased risk for sustained 
abuse. VFC made important adjustments to keep the program accessible 
during this unprecedented time. They moved largely to a telemedicine model, 
scheduling, and conducting all appointments by phone utilizing blocked 
numbers to communicate with patients to schedule safe times and locations to 
hold calls. For patients who had no privacy and no ability to receive personal 
calls, in-person visits to the Clinic were scheduled which could be explained as 
medical appointments. The Clinic staff reserved one day a week for these in-
person meetings. 
Case Study: 
One patient, a middle-aged immigrant woman with adult children, had been 
chronically verbally, emotionally, and psychologically abused for decades, 
experiencing extensive coercive manipulation. She was referred to domestic 
violence counseling support by one of VFC’s former volunteers, and after 
enrolling in the Clinic as a patient she was immediately connected to a 
Domestic Violence Specialist for domestic violence counseling. VFC designed 
her counseling with utmost thought for her safety. Her sessions took place when 
she was at work, and attention was paid to safely crafting an exit plan and 
ensuring the welfare of her pets. Through counseling, she has begun to re-
establish her self-worth and acknowledge the abuse she experienced. Now 
stronger, she ultimately made the decision to leave her marriage and live on her 
own. She has subsequently been granted a divorce and continues to work with 
the Domestic Violence Specialist to build her self-worth, heal, learn how to set 
boundaries, and maintain her personal safety. 
CHILDREN’S UNIT 
Rady Children’s Hospital-San Diego is a nonprofit, 511-bed pediatric-care facility 
dedicated to excellence in care, research, and teaching and is a Subrecipient 
of the Child Abuse Treatment (AT) Program. The AT Program provides funding for 
comprehensive psychotherapy services to children victimized by abuse and/or 
2 



--- PAGE 6 ---
 
 
 
 
   
  
  
   
  
 
 
    
  
  
  
  
 
   
  
    
 
  
  
   
   
 
   
  
  
   
 
   
  
   
 
  
 
  
neglect. Psychotherapy services for family members and/or caregivers of child 
victims are also available to help with the treatment of the child. 
Case Study: 
An 11-year-old child with a history of trauma exposure came to therapy after 
witnessing a shooting outside of her home. The child’s mother tried to perform 
life support assistance to the teenage shooting victim while she observed. The 
victim later died. Following this incident, the child experienced post-traumatic 
symptoms, including nightmares, heightened anxiety, hypervigilance, 
dissociative symptoms, and fear of being separated from her mother. A 
therapist began working with the child who quickly engaged and was very 
open about communicating the trauma and her symptoms. Goals of treatment 
included decreasing the child’s anxiety, processing the trauma she witnessed, 
and helping her develop coping skills to deal with triggers and trauma 
reminders. The therapist engaged the child in completing Trauma Focused 
Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (TF-CBT) to work through the trauma and practice 
the skills learned in TF-CBT. Now that she is nearing the end of treatment, the 
child has reported a decrease in her overall level of anxiety since services 
started. She also reported that she has been able to sleep in her own room 
again, which she has not done since the shooting occurred. She has also 
expressed that she is able to tolerate trauma reminders better, such as when she 
hears loud noises. In addition, the child has learned several techniques to 
manage her anxiety, such as deep breathing, using sensory tools, and 
progressive muscle relaxation. 
LAW ENFORCEMENT & COUNTY VICTIM SERVICES UNIT 
The Sacramento Internet Crimes Against Children (ICAC) Task Force focused 
efforts to increase resources towards proactive investigative efforts to combat 
child sexual abuse. They started the year with an Undercover Concepts and 
Techniques class as a beginning for Operation Secret Admirer. This operation 
was a multi-agency undercover operation to combat online-facilitated sexual 
exploitation of children, which led to the arrest of 18 suspects. The second 
operation was the two-week joint 2023 Cybertip Operation, which focused on 
investigating Cybertips from the National Center for Missing and Exploited 
Children. The Sacramento ICAC received 13,817 Cybertips in 2023. During this 
operation, 29 agencies participated in 37 search warrants related to Cybertips-
23 suspects were arrested. The final operation was Operation Monster Mash, a 
two-week operation that focused on suspects that were downloading Child 
Sexual Abuse Material (CSAM) to their personal devices and computers. Many 
of the targets were found in possession of horrendous CSAM files that depicted 
sexual images of infants and toddlers that were forced to engage in sexual 
abuse, including some that involved bondage, discipline, dominance and 
3 



--- PAGE 7 ---
 
  
  
 
  
  
 
   
   
   
 
    
    
 
    
    
 
   
 
    
   
  
    
      
    
    
  
   
 
    
 
   
 
 
  
 
    
submission, and sadomasochism. The operation resulted in 12 arrests and one 
pending arrest warrant. 
During the year, they continued their efforts in promoting a Northern California 
regional training center for forensic, internet crimes, and wellness training to 
service their 115 affiliated agencies. 
Case Study: 
During April 2023, a registered sex offender began communicating with an 
undercover (UC) Sacramento ICAC Task Force detective who posed as a 13-
year-old child. The perpetrator had previous convictions in 2014 for sending 
pornography to a minor and in 2016 for communicating with a minor to commit 
a sex offense and attempted lewd and lascivious acts with a child under 14. The 
perpetrator offered to meet with the UC detective and offered take her to get a 
tattoo “somewhere sexy” on her body. The perpetrator offered to teach the UC 
detective about sex and described in graphic detail sexual experiences with 
older people when he was her age. The perpetrator informed her he enjoyed 
role playing and pretending his sexual partners are minors; encouraged her to 
view pornography; provided graphic search terms to seek pornography; 
encouraged her to conceal their communication from her mother and to sneak 
out to meet with him and come to his residence; and sent explicit pictures and 
videos of himself to the UC detective. The perpetrator suddenly stopped 
communicating with the UC detective and did not return correspondence. 
ICAC detectives coordinated with a local sex offender registration unit and 
determined the perpetrator had moved out of his residence, purchased a 
different vehicle, changed his telephone number, and was preparing to move 
out of state.  On June 7, 2023, ICAC detectives intercepted the perpetrator just 
prior to him leaving California.  The perpetrator was arrested for three separate 
violations and was sentenced eight years in prison. 
DOMESTIC VIOLENCE UNIT 
The California Partnership to End Domestic Violence (Partnership), contracted 
with RALLY, an advocacy company, to create and disburse a campaign to 
further public awareness about the availability of gun violence restraining orders 
through the Gun Violence Restraining Order Outreach (GO) Program. The 
Partnership was able to conduct this outreach via various communication 
methods, such as billboards, social media, and in-person events. By utilizing 
domestic violence shelters as Second Tier Subawards, they were able to ensure 
outreach efforts were conducted statewide to reach domestic violence victims 
and marginalized communities. 
Additionally, a previously funded Subrecipient of the Domestic Violence 
Assistance (DV) Program ceased business operations creating fund availability 
for new Subrecipients to join the DV Program. Through a competitive Request for 
Proposal (RFP) process, three new Subrecipients that focus on providing 
4 



--- PAGE 8 ---
 
     
   
  
 
  
  
  
  
   
   
   
   
   
  
  
   
 
 
    
    
  
 
  
   
   
   
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
 
culturally specific domestic violence services to marginalized communities were 
able to be funded, for an overall total of 104 funded Subrecipients. 
Lastly, the program formerly known as the Equality in Prevention and Services for 
Domestic Abuse (GL) Program, was renamed the Equality in Domestic Violence 
Prevention and Services (EX) Program. This Program also released a competitive 
RFP and is funding three Subrecipients that provide direct domestic violence 
services to the LGBTQIA+ community. 
HOUSING UNIT 
Lake Family Resource Center is a local non-profit organization serving all of Lake 
County, providing services in child and youth development, parenting 
education and personal development, family and community violence 
prevention, intervention, treatment, and health & wellness. Lake Family 
Resource Center is a Subrecipient of the Domestic Violence Housing First (XD) 
Program. The XD Program provides victims/survivors of domestic violence with 
safe, permanent housing, and ongoing trauma-informed supportive services 
tailored to address the individual needs of each victim/survivor that allows them 
to choose how to best rebuild their lives. 
Case Study: 
A victim of domestic violence and her three children were referred to Lake 
Family Resource Center because she was fleeing from her abuser, who is also 
the father of her children. She was physically, emotionally, and financially 
abused and did not want to continue in the relationship. She also had a long 
history of previous abuse and childhood trauma which she still struggles with. 
The client was employed and was working on getting her medical assistant 
certification. Once she earned her certification, it increased her pay allowing 
her to bring more money into her household. She had been staying in Lake 
Family Resource Center’s emergency shelter and through the XD Program, she 
was connected to a landlord and was able to secure a large enough home to 
meet the needs of her family. A Family Advocate helped her create a budget 
and set a goal of saving one thousand dollars or more, which was achieved in a 
few months. She received therapy services to support her in her healing. Other 
supportive services from the XD Program included biweekly case management, 
transportation assistance, and financial assistance. The client was able to 
purchase a car through a local dealership, however, a few months later the 
vehicle began having issues. The Family Advocate helped her create a 
stabilization plan, which included rental assistance for six months and assistance 
with the car repair costs. The client continued with her financial goals and 
savings and maintained her employment. The XD Program assisted with the car 
repair costs and she can now drive herself to work and provide reliable 
transportation for her children.

HUMAN TRAFFICKING & LEGAL ASSISTANCE UNIT 
Justice At Last is a mobile legal practice, with satellite offices throughout the San 
Francisco Bay Area, exclusively serving human trafficking survivors. They provide 
comprehensive support in the following legal areas: criminal records clearance, 
immigration relief, employment rights, restraining orders, emergency custody, 
youth law, debt relief, and identity theft, and are a Subrecipient of the Human 
Trafficking Victim Assistance Program. 
Case Study: 
Justice At Last supported a male foreign-national labor trafficking survivor with 
crime victim’s rights representation and immigration relief. The client was 
prosecuted at the federal level for crimes he was forced to commit by his 
traffickers and Justice at Last represented him as his victims’ rights attorney. They 
collaborated with his defense attorney to obtain a reduced sentence through 
extensive advocacy to show that he was a victim of trafficking. However, on the 
day he was meant to be released from federal custody, he was detained by 
immigration officials and deportation proceedings were started. Despite limited 
means of communication with the client and facing an accelerated 
deportation timeline, Justice At Last ensured that their client would be safe from 
immediate deportation. They worked with several community partners to file a 
petition in the Ninth Circuit Court of Appeal to stay his deportation order. Justice 
At Last also worked with a congressmember to address the difficulties in 
accessing the client while he was detained. As a result of this collaboration, the 
client is temporarily safe from being returned to his home country, where he 
would be at great risk of persecution and re-trafficking. 
SEXUAL ASSAULT UNIT 
To continue to address the significant impact of COVID-19 on sexual assault 
service programs 103 Subrecipients funded through the Rape Crisis (RC) 
Program, Campus Sexual Assault (CT) Program, and the Victim Advocacy in 
Detention Facilities (KA) Program were able to utilize augmented funds through 
the Family Violence Prevention and Services Act (FVPSA) American Rescue Plan 
(ARP) COVID -19 Rape Crisis Centers and Sexual Assault Programs (FVSA) 
Supplemental Funding Program. The purpose of these funds is to assist with the 
transition to virtual/remote services for rape crisis centers, sexual assault 
programs, tribal programs, and culturally specific programs that provided crisis 
services, support services, and assistance to survivors of sexual assault and 
support the increased emergency needs of sexual assault survivors as a result of 
the COVID-19 public health emergency.

UNDERSERVED VICTIMS UNIT 
The Underserved Victims Unit continues to fund victim assistance service 
programs to various underserved populations throughout California. 
For fiscal year 2023-24, the Underserved Victims Unit distributed approximately 
$28 million via eight programs to assist unserved/underserved victim populations 
throughout California. The three largest funded programs are the 
Unserved/Underserved Advocacy and Outreach (UV) Program, The Innovative 
Response to Marginalized Victims (KI) Program, and the Elder Abuse (XE) 
Program. The Underserved Victims Unit also supports tribal programs: Tribal Court 
Advocates (KT) Program, Native American Domestic Violence and Sexual 
Assault (DS) Program, and Native American Child Abuse Treatment (NA) 
Program. 
The UV Program provides access to culturally appropriate victim services for 
unserved/underserved victims of crime. This Program receives a total of 
$14,767,950 to serve unserved/underserved victims. 
The KI Program supports innovative projects to provide services to marginalized 
victims of crime and victims of poly-victimization. The Program focuses on 
providing direct services to young males of color, victims of gang violence, 
refugees, low wage workers (janitorial, service industry, etc.), transgender, and 
foster care youth. This Program just completed a competitive process that 
distributed a total of $4,931,960 to 23 Subrecipients. 
The XE Program focuses on enhancing the safety of elder and dependent adult 
victims of abuse, as well as, bridging the gap between elder justice service 
providers and victim service providers. The Program supports the elderly and 
dependent adult communities by improving their ability to access services, 
identifying their needs, and improving the delivery of services to these 
populations. The Program just completed a competitive process that distributed 
$2,555,930 to 12 Subrecipients. 
VICTIM WITNESS & PROSECUTION UNIT 
Cal OES provides Victims of Crime Act (VOCA) Victim Assistance Formula Grant 
Program funds to 37 Child Advocacy Centers (CACs) in California through the 
Child Advocacy Center (KC) Program. The purpose of the KC Program is for 
CACs to provide direct victim services to child abuse victims/survivors and their 
families through a multidisciplinary team approach. These services include 
victim/survivor- centered, trauma-informed forensic interviews, advocacy, direct 
or referral to therapy/counseling, and referrals for medical exams. 
The KC Program makes an indelible difference in the lives of abused children 
across California. The KC Program expands trauma-informed child abuse 
services and interventions statewide. CACs are unique organizations statutorily 
7 



--- PAGE 11 ---
 
 
    
 
  
 
 
  
 
   
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
mandated to coordinate government agencies investigating child abuse cases; 
including police, prosecution, and child welfare professionals such as doctors 
and therapists trained to treat child abuse victims/survivors. VOCA funds 
awarded to CACs have increased and improved response to child abuse 
allegations to ensure that children receive prompt and improved victim/survivor 
centered services. This Program ensures a greater number of abused children in 
California receive access to immediate coordinated intervention and healing 
services. 
One of the most beneficial aspects of the KC Program is that it allows CACs to 
utilize funding to meet the specific needs of their unique community. All funds 
are spent with the goal of reducing trauma to abused children. Some of the 
significant improvements include, but are not limited to: 
• Creation of CACs in underserved communities to ensure that children in
these areas in California receive the support they need when they are
most vulnerable.
• Hiring dedicated, specially trained, forensic interviewers who conduct
trauma-informed, developmentally appropriate interviews with child
abuse victims/survivors.
• Increasing the number of CACs able to provide counseling and
advocacy services to victims/survivors of child abuse and decreasing the
wait time for therapy services.
• Increasing language access services by employing bi-lingual staff to
provide children and families services.
The KC Program has improved service provisions and enabled communities in 
California to better respond to the needs of child abuse victims/survivors. 
8

VICTIM SERVICES MAJOR FUND SOURCE DESCRIPTIONS
1. Victims of Crime Act (VOCA) Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (Formula
Grant Program)
T
he VOCA Formula Grant Program is authorized by the Victims of Crime Act of
1984 (34 U.S.C. § 20103 (a) and (b)).
VOCA authorizes federal financial assistance to states through the VOCA 
Formula Grant Program for the purpose of supporting eligible crime victim 
assistance programs that: 1) respond to the emotional and physical needs 
of victims of crime; 2) help primary and secondary victims of crime to 
stabilize their lives after a victimization; 3) help victims of crime understand 
and participate in the criminal justice system; and 4) provide victims of 
crime with a measure of safety and security. Crime victim is defined as any 
person who has suffered physical, sexual, financial, or emotional harm as a 
result of the commission of a crime. 
2. Services*Training*Officers* Prosecutors (STOP) Violence Against Women (VAW)
Formula Grant Program
The STOP VAW Formula Grant Program was authorized in 1994 through the
Violence Against Women Act(VAWA). VAWA, Public Law 103-322 was
reauthorized in 2000, 2005 and most recently in 2013. The STOP Formula Grant
Program is administered by the Office on Violence Against Women, Office of
Justice Programs, U.S. Department of Justice.
The STOP Formula Grant Program promotes the development and the 
implementation of effective, victim-centered law enforcement, 
prosecution, and court strategies to address violent crimes against women 
and the development and enhancement of victim services in cases 
involving violent crimes against women. States and communities are 
encouraged to restructure and strengthen the criminal justice system 
response to domestic violence, sexual assault, and stalking, drawing upon 
the experience of all participants in the system. 
3. The Family Violence Prevention and Services/Domestic Violence Shelter and
Supportive Services (FVPS) Grant
The FVPS Grant is authorized by 42 U.S.C. § 10401, Family Violence Prevention
and Services Act.
The Family Violence Prevention and Services/Domestic Violence Shelter 
and Supportive Services grant, administered through the Administration 
on Children, Youth and Families (ACYF) Family and Youth Services Bureau  
12 



--- PAGE 16 ---
 
  
 
 
     
  
     
  
  
  
 
   
 
 
   
 
  
  
 
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
(FYSB), is designed to assist states in their efforts to support the establishment, 
maintenance, and expansion of programs and projects to: 1) prevent incidents 
of family violence, domestic violence, and dating violence; 2) provide 
immediate shelter, supportive services, and access to community-based 
programs for victims of family violence, domestic violence, or dating violence, 
and their dependents; and 3) provide specialized services for children exposed 
to family violence, domestic violence, or dating violence including victims who 
are members of underserved populations (45 CFR § 1370.10(a)). 
4. Children’s Justice Act (CJA) Grants to States Program
The CJA Grants to State Program is authorized by Section 107(a) of the Child
Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (P.L. 111-320).
The purpose of the CJA Grant is to assist States in developing, establishing 
and operating programs designed to improve: (1) the assessment and 
investigation of suspected child abuse and neglect cases, including cases 
of suspected child sexual abuse and exploitation, in a manner that limits 
additional trauma to the child and the child’s family; (2) the assessment 
and investigation of cases of suspected child abuse-related fatalities and 
suspected child neglect-related fatalities; (3) the investigation and 
prosecution of cases of child abuse and neglect, including child sexual 
abuse and exploitation; and (4) the assessment and investigation of cases 
involving children with disabilities or serious health-related problems who 
are suspected victims of child abuse or neglect. 
5. Sexual Assault Services Formula Program (SASP)-specific to the RC Program
The Sexual Assault Services Formula Program (SASP) is authorized by 34 U.S.C.
§12511. SASP was created by the Violence Against Women and Department of
Justice Reauthorization Act of 2005, as amended by the technical amendments
to that act, and is the first federal funding stream solely dedicated to the
provision of direct intervention and related assistance for victims of sexual assault.
The SASP Formula Program directs grant dollars to states and territories to 
assist them in supporting rape crisis centers and other nonprofit, 
nongovernmental organizations or tribal programs that provide core 
services, direct intervention, and related assistance to victims of sexual 
assault. Rape crisis centers and other nonprofit and tribal organizations, such 
as dual programs providing both sexual violence and domestic violence 
intervention services, play a vital role in assisting sexual assault victims 
through the healing process, as well as assisting victims through the medical, 
criminal justice, and social support systems. Funds provided through the 
SASP Formula Program are designed to supplement other funding sources 
directed at addressing sexual assault at the state and territorial level. 
13 



--- PAGE 17 ---
 
   
 
  
 
  
 
6. State General Fund (SGF)
The California State Budget Act authorizes funding, in statute, each fiscal year to
specific programs. These programs support services for victims including, but not
limited to, housing for homeless youth, legal resources and information, family
violence prevention, and training and technical assistance.
7. State Penalty Fund (SPF)
The State Penalty Fund was created as a depository for assessments on specified
fines, penalties, and forfeitures imposed and collected by the courts for criminal
offenses and used to support victim services programs, amongst others. These
State funds can be used for match requirements following the provisions of the
Subrecipient Handbook, Section 9.060.
14 



--- PAGE 18 ---
    | 2024 
 
    
        
     
     
           
         
       
      
 
   
 
 
    
   
  
  
  
  
 
  
 
BEST PRACTICE INITIAL CHILD INTERVIEW TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Best Practice Initial Child Interview Training (VQ) Program is to 
educate first responders on how to properly conduct a forensically-sound, 
trauma-informed victim interview during an initial child sexual abuse 
investigation to ensure successful prosecution. 
This is accomplished through the creation of three training videos: a Commission 
on Peace Officer Standards and Training certified video for law enforcement; a 
video specific to Child Protective Service personnel; and one for the broader 
category of first responder mandated reporters. These training videos are to be 
made available statewide. 
The VQ Program is supported through Children’s Justice Act (CJA) Grant 
Funds. 
Program/Component: 0385.902

BULLYING & VIOLENCE IN SCHOOL ADVOCACY PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Bullying and Violence in School Advocacy (XB) Program is to 
provide counseling, advocacy, and other support services to children/teenagers 
who have been victimized by bullying and/or school violence. 
Required components of the XB Program include counseling, advocacy, and 
support services. Advocacy and support services may include, but are not limited 
to: 
• Anonymous tip line
• Criminal justice advocacy
• Crisis intervention
• Safety planning
• Parental/caregiver support to aid in the child/teenager’s recovery
The XB Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

CALIFORNIA ADVANCING THE PRISON RAPE ELIMINATION ACT PROGRAM 
| 2024  
The purpose of the California Advancing the Prison Rape Elimination Act (AP) 
Program is t
o enhance and increase services for victims of sexual assault in state and 
local correctional facilities. The AP Program allows Subrecipients to work with the 
California Department of Corrections and Rehabilitation (CDCR) to renew/secure 
formal partnerships between all CDCR facilities and their local Rape Crisis Centers 
(RCCs). 
The components of the AP Program include, but are not limited to: securing 
operational Memorandums of Understanding between RCCs and CDCR facilities to 
ensure victims have access to a Rape Crisis Counselor for accompaniment/advocacy 
services; providing training and technical assistance to RCCs; and establishing 
Second-Tier Subawards to administer financial support to participating RCCs. 
The AP Program is supported with funds through the Services*Training*Officers* 
Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161

CALIFORNIA CASA ASSOCIATION TRAINING AND RECRUITMENT 
PROGRAM
The purpose of the California CASA Association Training and Recruitment (KR) 
Program is to increase services to children in foster care by recruiting Court 
Appointed Special Advocate (CASA) volunteers statewide and by providing the 
required training to CASA volunteers. 
Required components of the KR Program include, but are not limited to: 
ensuring the training curriculum for CASA volunteers is current; conducting a 
minimum of three statewide trainings; implementing statewide marketing efforts 
to recruit CASA volunteers; and providing technical assistance to the CASA 
network, as needed. 
The KR Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) 
– Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA).
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 SGF-VCGF FY21 $ 133,287 
VOCA FFY22 $ 216,713 
FY 2023-24 VOCA FFY22 $ 350,000 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
2022-23 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
70 Number of regional trainings conducted (live and virtual). 
2,833 Number of CASA volunteers trained. 
24 Number of outreach/volunteer recruitment events held. 
California CASA Association partnered with LanguageLine Solutions to provide 
on-demand 24/7/365 access to translation and interpretation services for the 
statewide CASA network. This was made available as part of California CASA 
Association’s commitment to foster inclusivity and diversity throughout the CASA 
programs. Live, conversation translation services are offered via phone for 
immediate assistance when working with children and families. Written 
translation services for print and digital content, with access to 290 languages, 
are offered upon request. Through the use of LanguageLine Solutions, CASA 
staff and volunteers increase their support for non-English speaking children and 
caregivers, reducing one of the main barriers to assigning a volunteer to a child 
in need.

CALIFORNIA VICTIM WITNESS ADVOCATE TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the California Victim Witness Advocate Training (TP) Program is to 
design curriculum and provide high-quality, statewide training to Victim Witness 
(VW) Program Advocates, Supervisors, Coordinators, and allied professionals. 
Pursuant to Penal Code §13835.10, the TP Program establishes statewide minimum 
selection and training standards for VW Program Advocates and administrative 
personnel who provide direct victim services. 
Required components of the TP Program include the following trainings: 
40-hour Entry Level Advocate Academy, Advanced Advocate Academy,
Management/Supervisor Academy, and Train-the-Trainer courses. Upon request,
the TP Program provides ongoing technical assistance to VW Centers statewide.
The TP Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program Components: 0385.451, 0385.452

CALIFORNIA’S CHILDREN’S JUSTICE ACT EVALUATOR PROGRAM
The purpose of the California’s Children’s Justice Act Evaluator (CJ) Program is 
to support CJA-funded programs by developing assessments and tools that will 
be used to assist with meeting program goals and identifying recommendations 
that will fill program needs or gaps. 
Required components of the CJ Program include: evaluating CJA-funded 
Programs to identify the effectiveness of the programs and updating the 
Electronic Suspected Child Abuse Reporting System Guidance Document, as 
needed. 
The CJ Program is supported with funds through federal Children's Justice Act 
Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 CJA0 FFY19 $ 16,666 
CJA0 FFY20 $ 83,334 
FY 2023-24 CJA0 FFY21 $ 50,000 
CJA0 FFY22 $ 50,000 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
2022-23 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
Northwest Professional Consortium Research, Inc. (NPC Research) worked with 
the Subrecipients of the Minor’s Counsel Program to collect post-ruling data 
from judicial officers and attorneys, as well as collected data from each 
Subrecipient’s court case management systems, for evaluation. NPC Research 
conducted interviews with the Minor’s Counsel Program staff and developed a 
report with the summary of the interviews, including challenges, lessons learned, 
and recommendations. 
NPC Research facilitated calls with the Electronic Suspected Child Abuse 
Reporting System Program (eSCARS) Subrecipients. Information was gathered 
to evaluate the progress for each Subrecipient and NPC Research developed 
a report summarizing the profiles of each Subrecipient, their progress, and 
future plans. NPC Research also collected feedback from each eSCARS 
Subrecipient to include in the next revision of the Electronic Suspected Child 
Abuse Reporting System Guidance Document. 
NPC Research analyzed survey data and prepared summary reports for the 
Child Abduction Training Program.

CAMPUS SEXUAL ASSAULT PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Campus Sexual Assault (CT) Program is to maintain a 
comprehensive, coordinated, community response that is victim-centered, 
enhances victim safety, provides confidential services to sexual assault victims, 
holds offenders accountable, and includes comprehensive outreach activities 
at institutions of higher education. 
This is accomplished through the development of clear policies and protocols 
on the handling of reports of sexual assault, ongoing trainings on sexual assault 
and sensitivity for all campus staff, and specialized services for victims. A Sexual 
Assault Counselor from a local Cal OES-funded Rape Crisis Center (as defined in 
Evidence Code §1035.2) is located on campus, promoting the availability of the 
services, and providing training to students, Police/Safety Officers, campus 
investigatory personnel, staff, and faculty. 
The CT Program is currently supported with funds through federal Family 
Violence Prevention and Services Act American Rescue Plan COVID-19 Rape 
Crisis Centers and Sexual Assault Programs Supplemental Funding (FVSA), 
federal Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula 
Grant Program (STOP), and federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance 
Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.154, 0385.161, 0385.451

CHILD ABDUCTION TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Child Abduction Training (AC) Program is to collaborate 
with the Child Abduction Task Force to provide statewide training to 
multidisciplinary professionals within the child abduction field. The AC Program 
also manages the Child Abduction Task Force website by keeping it updated 
with training information and relevant resources about child abduction. 
Required components of the AC Program include: 
• Collaborating with the members of the Child Abduction Task Force to
facilitate Child Abduction Intervention and Resource Training, as well to
facilitate Child Abduction Simulated Exercises;
• Updating the Child Abduction Website with resources and information for
professionals who may come into contact with missing or abducted
children and/or their families;
• Surveying participants at the end of each training session to ensure topics
and information remain relevant; and
• Providing oversight of the Child Abduction Task Force to include
appointments and dismissals of Task Force members and managing travel
arrangements and expenses for travel related to training and meetings.
The AC Program is supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice 
Act Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902

CHILD ABUSE TREATMENT PROGRAM
The purpose of the Child Abuse Treatment (AT) Program is to provide 
comprehensive psychotherapy services to children victimized by abuse and/or 
neglect. Psychotherapy services for family members and/or caregivers of child 
victims are also available to help with the treatment of the child. 
Required components of the AT Program include: 
• Providing free comprehensive treatment services to child victims and
secondary victims to include psychotherapy and/or culturally-centered
therapy, crisis intervention, group counseling, case management,
treatment plan meetings with parents/caregivers, criminal justice
orientation, transportation services to psychotherapy and/or criminal
justice-related activities, and outreach.
• Providing services to child victims who do not have access to services due
to lack of available resources, lack of mental health insurance, and/or non-
eligibility for other programs or services.
• Prioritizing services to child victims who are underserved or are dependents
of the court and involved in the child welfare system and
• Assisting child victims with California Victim Compensation Board claims.
The AT Program is supported with federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance 
Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451

CHILD ADVOCACY CENTER PROGRAM | 2024 
The purpose of the Child Advocacy Center (KC) Program is for Child Advocacy 
Centers (CACs) to provide direct victim services to child abuse victims/survivors 
and their families through a multidisciplinary team approach. These services 
must include victim-centered, trauma-informed forensic interviews, advocacy, 
direct or referral to therapy/counseling, and referrals for medical exams. 
California Penal Code §11166.4 states “each county may use a children’s 
advocacy center to implement a coordinated multidisciplinary response, 
pursuant to Section 18961.7 of the Welfare and Institutions Code, to investigate 
reports involving child physical or sexual abuse, exploitation, or maltreatment. A 
county that utilizes a CAC to coordinate its multidisciplinary team shall require 
the CAC to meet standards pursuant to California Penal Code §11166.4, part b.” 
Required components of the KC Program include, but are not limited to: 
a multidisciplinary team consisting of law enforcement, child protection, 
prosecution, mental health, medical, and victim advocacy professionals who 
work together to help children heal from abuse; a dedicated child-focused 
setting designed to provide a safe, comfortable, neutral place where forensic 
interviews and other CAC services may be provided for children and families; 
direct forensic interviewer and advocacy services; and case review and 
tracking. 
The KC Program is supported with the following funds: Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451

CHILD SEXUAL ABUSE TRAINING AND TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 
| 2024 
The purpose of the Child Sexual Abuse Training and Technical Assistance 
(NC/SC) Program is to provide training and technical assistance to service 
providers who work directly with child sexual abuse victims or who handle child 
sexual abuse cases. 
The NC/SC Program addresses the requirements of Welfare and Institutions 
code §18275 by funding training centers, one in Northern California (Northern 
Child Sexual Abuse and Technical Assistance Center) and one in Southern 
California (Southern Child Sexual Abuse and Technical Assistance Center). The 
training centers are responsible for developing training programs for interagency 
teams of service providers throughout California, including law enforcement, 
social services, health care providers, and mental health providers. Service 
providers who receive the training will then provide training in their communities. 
Required components of the NC/SC Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Providing training on sexual abuse issues
• Providing up to four Child Forensic Interview Training classes
• Providing training topics that cover the issues and needs of child sexual
abuse victims with disabilities
• Ensuring training is culturally and linguistically competent
• Providing course certification to meet licensing and/or continuing education
needs of the attending professionals
• Using training assessments that evaluate the trainers and the effectiveness of
the training curriculum
The NC/SC Program is supported with the following funds: State General 
Funds (SGF) – Child Abuse Prevention/Training (CSAP), State General 
Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), and federal Victims of 
Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.353, 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

CHILD SEXUAL ABUSE TREATMENT PROGRAM
The purpose of the Child Sexual Abuse Treatment (CS) Program is to provide 
comprehensive services to children under the age of 18 who are victims of 
sexual abuse and/or sexual exploitation. Services may also be provided to the 
child victim’s family members to aid in the child’s recovery. 
Required components of the CS Program include: 
• Crisis intervention
• Individual counseling
• Referrals to other services
• Follow-up support
The CS Program is supported with the following funds: State General 
Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), State Penalty 
Funds (SPF) – Child Sexual Abuse and Exploitation (CSAE), federal Family 
Violence Prevention and Services Act (FVPSA) American Rescue Plan (ARP) 
COVID-19 Rape Crisis Centers and Sexual Assault Programs (FVSA), and federal 
Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.154, 0385.354, 0385.451, 0385.452

COUNTY VICTIM SERVICES PROGRAM | 2024  
The purpose of the County Victim Services (XC) Program is to assess the existing 
victim service structure, identify needs, and fill gaps in victim services in all 
counties statewide and in the City of Los Angeles. 
Services provided through the XC Program must: provide victim-centered, 
trauma-informed direct victim services to respond to the emotional, 
psychological, and/or physical needs of marginalized crime victims; assist victims 
to understand and participate in the criminal justice system; restore a measure of 
security and safety for the victim; and/or assist victims with compensation claims 
through the California Victim Compensation Board. 
The XC Program is supported with the following funds: State General Fund 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime 
Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

COURT APPOINTED SPECIAL ADVOCATES PROGRAM
The purpose of the Court Appointed Special Advocates (KS) Program is to fund 
Court Appointed Special Advocate (CASA) programs in California to provide 
more children in foster care with a CASA volunteer. 
CASAs are trained community volunteers appointed by juvenile court judges to 
advocate for children who are placed into the protective custody of the child 
welfare system due to abuse or neglect. CASAs review court facts and 
documents pertaining to the child’s case and submit formal reports to the judge 
at every scheduled hearing/review. The CASA monitors the child’s case to 
assure that the judicial and child welfare systems are moving ahead to secure a 
safe, permanent home for the child, and that court-ordered services are 
provided to the child. 
Required components of the KS Program include: recruiting volunteers, training 
CASA volunteers pursuant to the Welfare & Institutions Code § 102(d) and 
103(a), and providing oversight, support, and supervision to CASA volunteers. 
The KS Program is supported with federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance 
Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

COURT EDUCATION AND TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Court Education and Training (CW) Program is to develop 
and provide trainings, seminars, publications, symposiums, and other programs 
designed to increase the knowledge of court personnel in cases involving 
domestic violence, sexual assault, stalking, and teen dating violence. These 
trainings are offered to both state and tribal court judges and all judicial officers. 
Required components of the CW Program include, but are not limited to: 
implementation of a training needs assessment, development and 
implementation of a training plan, curriculum development and ongoing 
revisions to ensure all curriculum provides judicial education specifically in family, 
criminal, juvenile, civil, and probate courts, and development and distribution of 
training materials. 
The CW Program is supported with the following fund: federal Services*Training 
*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP).
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 STOP FFY22 $ 768,597 
FY 2023-24 STOP FFY23 $ 768,597 
FY 2024-25 Unknown

violence, and human trafficking courses for judicial staff/total 
number of attendees.

CRISIS RESPONSE TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Crisis Response Training (CR) Program is the implementation 
of comprehensive crisis response training and technical assistance to local 
Victim Witness Assistance (VW) Centers and multi-disciplinary teams to establish 
response readiness to mass victimization crimes. 
Required components of the CR Program include: 
• Annual training needs assessment
• Curriculum development
• Regional and statewide Crisis Response trainings
• Train the Trainer trainings
• Mass Victimization (MV) Advocate Roundtable meetings
The CR Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of 
Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE AMERICAN RESCUE PLAN PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence American Rescue Plan (RP) Program is to 
guarantee the continuity of domestic violence services and/or response to 
COVID-19 by providing funding for non-shelter supportive services, prevention 
and/or prevention activities. 
Required components of the RP Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Individual and group counseling
• Crisis intervention
• Legal advocacy
• Advocacy and case management
• Childcare
• Peer support
• Gift cards
• Transportation services (gas cards, Uber and Lyft vouchers)
• Outreach activities
• Safety plan development
• Emergency preparedness efforts
The RP Program is supported with funds through the federal Family Violence 
Prevention and Services/Domestic Violence Shelter and Supportive Services Grant 
(FVPSA) – Supplemental Funding American Rescue Plan (ARP) of 2021. 
Program Component: 0385.150 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22* FVPA FFY21 $ 2,162,803 
FY 2022-23 One-time funded 
FY 2023-24 One-time funded 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
*The performance period is January 1, 2022 - December 31, 2023.

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Assistance (DV) Program is to provide 
comprehensive support, including emergency shelter to victims of domestic 
violence and their children, and local assistance to existing domestic violence 
service providers. The DV Program also provides support for the development 
and establishment of domestic violence services to unserved and underserved 
populations, including, but not limited to, rural areas, non-English speaking 
individuals, persons of color, and various geographical areas with limited access 
to services. 
Pursuant to California Penal Code §13823.15 -13823.16, required components of 
the DV Program include: 
• Twenty-four-hour crisis hotlines • Counseling for children
• Counseling (individual and peer • Household establishment
group) assistance
• Business centers • Transportation
• Emergency shelter for victims and • Criminal justice and social services
their children advocacy 
• Emergency food and clothing • Court accompaniment
• Emergency response to calls from • Medical advocacy and
law enforcement emergency response 
• Legal assistance with temporary • Local community service network
restraining orders and other
protective orders
The DV Program is supported with the following: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Domestic Violence (DVP0), federal Family Violence Prevention and Services 
Grant to States for Domestic Violence Shelters and Support Services Program 
(FVPS), and federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant 
Program (VOCA). 
This Program was augmented and extended through September of 2024. 
Program/Components: 0385.151, 0385.451

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE HOUSING FIRST PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Housing First (XD) Program is to provide 
victims/survivors of domestic violence with safe, permanent housing, and ongoing 
trauma-informed supportive services tailored to address the individual needs of 
each victim/survivor that allows them to choose how to best rebuild their lives.  
The XD Program is modeled after the Housing First Model, as defined in Welfare and 
Institutions (W&I) Code § 8255, to address the prevalence of homelessness in 
California. The Housing First Model is an approach to connect individuals and 
families experiencing homelessness quickly and successfully to permanent housing 
without preconditions and barriers to entry, such as sobriety, treatment, or service 
participation requirements. 
Required components of the XD Program include, but are not limited to: housing 
assistance, financial assistance, and supportive services for victims. Organizations 
that receive XD Program funding must also hire a minimum of one full-time 
equivalent advocate, who has met the requirements of a Domestic Violence 
Counselor as described in Evidence Code §1037.1(a)(1) as well as a minimum of 
one half-time equivalent advocate who serves as a Housing Coordinator. 
The XD Program is supported with funds through federal Victims of Crime Act Victim 
Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE MOBILE HEALTH CARE SERVICES PROGRAM
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Mobile Health Care Services (FO) Program is 
to provide domestic violence victims/survivors and their children access to COVID-19 
testing, vaccines, and primary health care through weekly mobile health care visits. 
To ensure comprehensive and essential healthcare for domestic violence 
victims/survivors and their children in all areas of California, Cal OES initially 
adopted a regional approach where Subrecipients coordinated services for all 
domestic violence shelters and programs within identified regions. The outcome of 
this approach resulted in 48 of the 58 counties statewide to remain unserved. 
Cal OES then re-released the Program to serve the remaining 48 counties. This 
combination of strategies more efficiently covered all areas of California. Services 
provided by the FO Program include, but are not limited to: COVID-19 vaccines; 
COVID-19 testing; primary health care (e.g. wellness checks, health exams, 
referrals); and post-acute COVID-19 care (e.g. education, rehabilitation, 
prevention, treatment, and support services for symptoms occurring after recovery 
from acute COVID-19 infection). 
The FO Program is funded through the federal Family Violence Prevention and 
Services Act (FVPSA) American Rescue Plan COVID-19 Testing, Vaccines, and 
Mobile Health Unit Supplemental Funding Program (FVCO). 
Program/Component: 0385.153 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23* FVCO FFY21 $ 20,461,942 
FY 2023-24* FVCO FFY21 $ 14,043,633 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
*Both performance periods span FY 2022-23 to FY 2024-25.

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE RESPONSE TEAM PROGRAM
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Response Team (VA) Program is to support 
agencies in building protocols and practices for a collaborative and community-
coordinated response to domestic violence. The VA Program builds the capacity of 
communities that demonstrate a need for greater collaboration and coordination 
between domestic violence service providers and law enforcement organizations. 
The required components for the VA Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Establishing law enforcement and advocacy liaisons;
• Improving domestic violence protocols;
• Ensuring prompt community response;
• Providing training, tools and materials;
• Creating a capacity building plan;
• Co-locating; and
• Entering into Operational Agreements/Second Tier Subawards with specific
agencies (e.g., local law enforcement, district attorney’s office[s]), and
assisting with California Victim Compensation Board Claims.
The VA Program is supported with federal Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors 
Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

ELDER ABUSE PROGRAM
The purpose of the Elder Abuse (XE) Program is to continue to enhance the 
safety of elder and dependent adult victims/survivors of crime by providing 
direct services to victims/survivors and bridging the gap between elder justice 
service providers and victim services providers. 
This is accomplished by the following: 
• Funding an Elder Abuse Victim Specialist solely dedicated to serving elder
and dependent adult victims/survivors;
• Providing/coordinating direct services for elder and dependent adult
victims/survivors;
• Expanding the communities’ capacity to service elder and dependent
adult victims/survivors;
• Ensuring that elder and dependent adult victims/survivors have access to
the criminal justice system; and
• Continuing to provide outreach awareness programs for victims/survivors,
mandated reporters of elder and dependent adult abuse, and others who
come into contact with these victims/survivors.
The XE Program is supported with funding through federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

ELECTRONIC SUSPECTED CHILD ABUSE REPORTING SYSTEM PROGRAM| 
The purpose of the Electronic Suspected Child Abuse Reporting System (ES) 
Program is to improve the system of sharing information for suspected child 
abuse cases by establishing an electronic Suspected Child Abuse Report (SCAR) 
system that will be used by district attorney offices, local law enforcement, and 
county child welfare services departments. 
Required components of the ES Program include: 
• Convening key stakeholders, including the district attorney office, local law
enforcement agencies, and the child welfare services department;
• Identifying the approach for building the electronic suspected child abuse
reporting system (eSCARS);
• Building and testing the new eSCARS;
• Developing a plan for rollout and sustainability; and
• Transitioning the current SCAR process to eSCARS.
The ES Program is supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice Act 
Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 CJA0 FFY20 $ 130,877 
CJA0 FFY21 $ 469,123 
FY 2023-24 CJA0 FFY21 $ 162,592 
CJA0 FFY22 $ 437,408 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
2022-23 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
Each Subrecipient has made considerable progress developing their eSCARS 
systems. They have also each engaged with the required agencies and have 
established policies and protocols for the use of eSCARS. The Subrecipients are 
working on implementation plans and training.

EMERGING FAMILY JUSTICE  CENTER PROGRAM  | 2024 
The purpose of the Emerging Family Justice Center (FE) Program is to support 
emerging Family Justice Centers (FJCs) to meet the criteria defined in California 
Penal Code §13750. 
A city, county, city and county, or community-based nonprofit organization may 
each establish a multiagency, multidisciplinary family justice center to assist 
victims of domestic violence, sexual assault, elder or dependent adult abuse, 
and human trafficking, to ensure that victims of abuse are able to access all 
needed services in one location in order to enhance victim safety, increase 
offender accountability, and improve access to services for victims of domestic 
violence, sexual assault, elder or dependent adult abuse, and human 
trafficking. 
The FE Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Family Justice 
Center (FJC0). 
Program/Component: 0385.921 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

**The performance period for this Program is April 1, 2022 – March 31, 2024.

EQUALITY IN DOMESTIC VIOLENCE PREVENTION AND SERVICES 
PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Equality in Domestic Violence Prevention and Services (EX) 
Program (formerly, the Equality in Prevention and Services for Domestic Abuse (GL) 
Program) is to maintain and increase domestic violence services for the lesbian, 
gay, bi-sexual, transgender, queer/questioning, Intersex and asexual (LGBTQIA+) 
communities that will improve access to culturally appropriate domestic violence 
education, prevention, outreach, and services for these unserved/underserved 
communities. 
Pursuant to California Penal Code §13823.17 the EX Program provides: 
• Crisis intervention (Initial and Ongoing);
• Legal advocacy;
• Participation in domestic violence multidisciplinary teams (including, but not
limited to, law enforcement, medical treatment facilities, and other service
providers);
• 24-hour crisis hotline;
• Business Centers;
• Counseling services (Individual and Peer Group);
• Legal assistance (Temporary Restraining Orders/Other Protective Orders and
Custody Disputes);
• Criminal Justice advocacy;
• Social Service advocacy;
• Educational workshops and publications;
• Community resources and referrals;
• Emergency shelter access; and
• Household establishment assistance.
The EX Program is supported with funds through the federal Family Violence 
Prevention and Services Grant to States for Domestic Violence Shelters and Support 
Services Program (FVPS). 
Program/Component: 0385.151, 0385.906 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 FVPS FFY22 $ 423,000 
EPSD FY22 $ 375,000 
FY 2023-24 FVPS FFY23 $ 423,000 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
EX PROGRAM

FAMILY JUSTICE CENTER PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Family Justice Center (FJ) Program is to support fully 
operational, multiagency, multidisciplinary Family Justice Centers (FJCs) 
pursuant to California Penal Code §§13750-13751. 
FJCs provide centralized access to services and support for victims/survivors and 
their children, to ensure that victims/survivors of abuse can access all needed 
services in one location in order to enhance victim/survivor safety, increase 
offender accountability, and improve access to services for one or more of the 
following: 
• Domestic violence
• Sexual assault
• Elder abuse
• Dependent adult abuse
• Human trafficking
The FJ Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Family Justice 
Center (FJC0 and FJC5). 
Program/Component: 0385.921 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

performance period for this Program is April 1, 2022 – March 31, 2024.

FARMWORKER WOMEN’S SEXUAL ASSAULT & DOMESTIC VIOLENCE 
PROGRAM
The purpose of the Farmworker Women’s Sexual Assault & Domestic Violence 
(FW) Program is to develop and implement activities aimed at addressing and 
combating the issues of sexual assault and domestic violence (SA/DV) among 
farmworker women who are low-income, monolingual or bilingual Spanish-
speaking. 
This is accomplished through the following: 
• Providing statewide outreach and education on SA/DV among
monolingual, bilingual Spanish-speaking, and low-income farmworker
women;
• Developing and incorporating two additional Chapters to expand services
across California;
• Providing training to agencies addressing SA/DV in the farmworker
community;
• Developing and continuously updating outreach materials focusing on the
special needs of farmworker women;
• Disseminating information to Cal OES-funded Rape Crisis and Domestic
Violence Subrecipients regarding the unique service provision needs of
farmworker women;
• Linking farmworker SA/DV victims to available resources;
• Coordinating and collaborating with Rape Crisis Centers, Domestic
Violence Shelters and Victim/Witness Programs in all Chapter areas;
• Coordinating and hosting community awareness and educational events,
addressing sexual assault and domestic violence, for farmworker women
from all Chapters; and
• Involving indigenous women in SA/DV awareness efforts and conducting
educational meetings in indigenous Mexican farmworker communities.
The FW Program is supported with funds through the federal 
Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant 
Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 STOP FFY20 $ 107,143 
STOP FFY21 $ 392,857 
FY 2023-24 STOP FFY23 $ 500,000

GUN VIOLENCE RESTRAINING ORDER OUTREACH PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Gun Violence Restraining Order Outreach (GO) Program is to 
inform domestic violence victims of the availability of gun violence restraining 
orders and how to petition for one through an outreach campaign tailored to 
domestic violence victims, including those most marginalized. The GO Program 
assists with the development of the materials, creates additional materials as 
needed, implements the campaign, and subawards funds to local domestic 
violence groups to conduct target outreach in their communities with emphasis on 
the most marginalized victims of domestic violence. 
The GO Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – (DVST). 
Program/Component: 0385.105

HOMELESS YOUTH AND EXPLOITATION PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Homeless Youth and Exploitation (HX) Program is to help 
homeless youth exit street life, with a focus on providing specialized services for 
youth experiencing sexual exploitation. Services provided under the HX Program 
include: access to food; shelter/housing; counseling; outreach services; 
screening/referrals for basic health needs; linkage to services offered by public and 
private agencies; long-term stabilization planning; and follow-up services. 
The HX Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Homeless Youth (HY00), State Penalty Fund (SPF) – Child Sexual Abuse & Exploitation 
(HY05), and Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Components: 0385.351, 0385.451, 0385.452

HOMELESS YOUTH EMERGENCY SERVICES AND HOUSING PROGRAM| 
The purpose of the Homeless Youth Emergency Services and Housing (YE) 
Program is to establish or expand access to a range of housing options and 
provide crisis intervention and stabilization services to homeless youth so the 
immediate crises they face can be resolved and they can focus on their future. 
During this performance period, YE Program Subrecipients were strongly 
encouraged to work collaboratively with local colleges to provide support, 
services, and housing options to homeless students. 
Pursuant to California Welfare and Institutions Code §13701, the YE Program 
provides: 
• Food/meals;
• Emergency shelter and a range of non-shelter housing options;
• Counseling to address the immediate emotional crisis or problems;
• Outreach services to locate youth experiencing homelessness;
• Screening and providing for basic health care needs;
• Linkage to other services offered by public and private agencies, such as
employment services, law enforcement agencies, social services, schools,
mental health and healthcare providers, and local victim/witness
assistance centers; and
• Long-term stabilization planning and follow-up services to ensure that family
reunification or placement outside the family is stable.
The YE Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Homeless Youth 
Emergency Services (HYES). 
Program/Component: 0385.355 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-26* SGF-HYES FY21 $ 38,000,000 
Unknown

there has been no additional funding allocated.

HOMELESS YOUTH EMERGENCY SERVICES PILOT PROGRAM
The purpose of the Homeless Youth Emergency Services Pilot (HY) Program is to 
expand crisis intervention and stabilization services to homeless youth so that the 
immediate crises these youth face can be resolved, and they can focus on their 
futures. 
Pursuant to California Welfare and Institutions Code §13701 the HY Program 
provides: 
• Food/meals and access to shelter
• Counseling to address the immediate emotional crisis or problem
• Outreach services such as drop-in facilities
• Screening and providing for basic health care needs
• Referrals to other services offered by public and private agencies, such as
employment services, law enforcement agencies, social services, schools,
mental health and healthcare providers, and local victim/witness
assistance centers
• Long-term stabilization planning, and follow-up services, to ensure that
family reunification or placement outside the family is stable.
The HY Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF)—Emergency 
Housing and Assistance Fund (EHAF). 
Program/Component: 0385.917

HUMAN TRAFFICKING ADVOCATE PROGRAM
The purpose of the Human Trafficking Advocate (HA) Program is to provide 
funding to Victim Witness Assistance Centers (Centers) so the Centers can hire 
or dedicate at least one half-time advocate who will provide comprehensive 
services to victims/survivors of human trafficking. Human trafficking 
victims/survivors include sex and labor trafficking. 
Required components of the HA Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Crisis intervention;
• Emergency assistance;
• Resource and referral assistance;
• Counseling/therapy;
• Property return;
• Orientation to the criminal justice system;
• Court escort;
• Outreach to criminal justice agencies;
• Community outreach and awareness;
• Case status/disposition;
• Notification of family/friends;
• Employer notification/intervention;
• Restitution advocacy; and
• Linkage to other services offered by public and private agencies.
The HA Program is supported with federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance 
Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451

HUMAN TRAFFICKING VICTIM ASSISTANCE PROGRAM
The purpose of the Human Trafficking Victim Assistance (HV) Program is to help 
human trafficking victims/survivors (sex trafficking and labor trafficking) recover 
from the trauma they experienced and assist them with reintegrating into 
society through comprehensive safety and supportive services using a trauma-
informed, culturally sensitive, victim/survivor-centered approach. 
Required components of the HV Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Twenty-four hour crisis hotline available for immediate crisis intervention and
assistance;
• Emergency shelter;
• Temporary housing/housing assistance;
• Emergency food and clothing;
• Transportation;
• Emergency financial assistance;
• Psychological support/individual counseling;
• Referrals to local community services; and
• Legal assistance.
Organizations that receive funding through the HV Program must ensure staff 
working with human trafficking victims qualify as Human Trafficking Caseworkers 
as described in Evidence Code §1038.2 and are trained according to the 
minimum requirements therein. 
Since fiscal year 2019-20, many HV Program Subrecipients shifted focus to better 
identify and serve victims of labor trafficking as well as sex trafficking. Examples 
have included additional training for victim service advocates and partner 
organizations (such as hospital/health clinic staff), co-locating human trafficking 
caseworkers in hospitals, clinics, and family justice centers, and the 
development of local multi-disciplinary teams with expertise in addressing the 
needs of labor trafficking victims. 
The HV Program is supported through State General Funds (SGF) – Human 
Trafficking Victim Assistance (HTVA). 
Program/Components: 0385.912 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 SGF-HTVA FY22 $ 20,000,000 
FY 2023-24 SGF-HTVA FY23 $ 27,000,000 
FY 2024-25 Unknown 
HV PROGRAM

INNOVATIVE RESPONSE TO MARGINALIZED VICTIMS PROGRAM
The purpose of the Innovative Response to Marginalized Victims (KI) Program is to 
implement new and unique methods to provide direct support services for 
victims/survivors of crime who are part of a marginalized population and to 
support innovative services for marginalized victims/survivors of crime. 
The KI Program supports the following victim/survivor groups: refugees, 
people/communities of color (identity-based communities that hold a primary 
racial or ethnic identity that describes shared racial/ethnic characteristics among 
community members, e.g., LatinX, Black Indigenous, and People of Color (BIPOC); 
persons with disabilities, low wage workers, LGBTIQA+, disadvantaged/foster 
youth, and gang violence. 
Required components of the KI Program include, but are not limited to: providing 
direct victim services to respond to the emotional, psychological, or physical 
needs of marginalized crime victims/survivors; providing survivor-driven advocacy; 
restoring a measure of safety and security for the victim/survivor; assisting 
victims/survivors to understand and participate in the criminal justice system; and 
assisting victims/survivors to stabilize their lives after victimization. 
The KI Program is supported with funding through the federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451

INTERNET CRIMES AGAINST CHILDREN PROGRAM
The purpose of the Internet Crimes Against Children (IC) Program is to ensure 
that local law enforcement agencies are adequately equipped to investigate 
cybercrimes that target youth; provide educational programs aimed at law 
enforcement officers, teachers, parents, and children; serve as a forensic 
resource to law enforcement agencies within each service area; pursue the 
prosecution of cases at the local, state, and federal level; and participate in 
nationally-coordinated investigative efforts. 
Required components include: conduct proactive investigations into 
technology crimes perpetrated against children and youth; serve as a forensic 
resource to affiliate agencies within the Task Force’s geographical service area; 
assist and enhance prosecutorial efforts at the local, state, and federal level; 
participate in law enforcement and community outreach activities to provide 
prevention, education, and training; formalize law enforcement partnerships 
through signed Operational Agreements; participate in nationally coordinated 
investigations; and assist with arrests for technology crimes perpetrated against 
children and youth. 
The IC Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Internet Crimes 
Against Children (ICAC). 
Program/Component: 0385.908 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

INTIMATE PARTNER VIOLENCE PREVENTION PROGRAM
The purpose of the Intimate Partner Violence Prevention (FD) Program is to 
prevent intimate partner violence, including teen dating violence, prioritize 
underserved populations within communities, and build the capacity of local 
organizations to do this work. 
Required components of the FD Program include: 
• Commitment of one full-time equivalent Prevention Staff to accomplish the
goals of the FD Program
• Implementation of a Prevention Program
• Development of an evaluation plan through the assistance of technical
assistance providers to determine how short-term, intermediate, and long
term outcomes and impact will be measured
• Modeling web-based training curriculum
• Resources for Voluntary Participation in Services
The FD Program is supported with the following: federal Family Violence 
Prevention and Services Grant to States for Domestic Violence Shelters and 
Support Services Program (FVPS), and federal 
Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant 
Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.151, 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING ALLOCATED

LAW ENFORCEMENT SPECIALIZED UNITS PROGRAM
The purpose of the Law Enforcement Specialized Units (LE) Program is to create 
or enhance specialized units that provide a coordinated response to victims of 
domestic violence and their children. 
The specialized units accomplish this through investigation, immediate victim 
advocacy, training for patrol officers and first responders, and the development 
of effective protocols and practices. In addition, each Subrecipient must 
contract with a local Cal OES-funded Domestic Violence Assistance (DV) 
Program Subrecipient for at least one full-time equivalent (FTE) Domestic 
Violence Counselor, pursuant to Evidence Code §1037.1(a)(1). 
The LE Program is supported with funds through the federal 
Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant 
Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

LAW ENFORCEMENT TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Law Enforcement Training (PO) Program is to train law 
enforcement personnel in the areas of sexual assault, domestic violence, 
stalking, and culturally specific dynamics as it relates to these crimes. 
Specific training courses provided include: 
• Domestic Violence for First Responders
• Sexual Assault for First Responders
• Domestic Violence for Public Safety Dispatchers
• Sexual Assault for Public Safety Dispatchers
• Institute of Criminal Investigation (ICI) Domestic Violence for Criminal
Investigators.
The PO Program is supported with funds through the federal 
Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant 
Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 STOP FFY21 $ 173,134 
STOP FFY22 $ 242,389

LEGAL ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Legal Assistance (XL) Program is to increase the availability of 
free comprehensive civil and legal assistance needed to effectively aid adult 
and youth victims/survivors of crime who are seeking relief in legal matters 
related to that crime. XL Program funds may not be used to provide criminal 
defense services. 
Comprehensive legal services should address the broad spectrum of legal issues 
that victims/survivors encounter and help promote economic independence for 
victims/survivors. Required components include, but are not limited to: legal 
representation provided by licensed attorneys, outreach to victims/survivors in 
rural or isolated communities, and using interpretation services to 
accommodate victims/survivors who have limited English proficiency. 
The XL Program is supported with funding from the federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

LEGAL TRAINING PROGRAM
The purpose of the Legal Training (LT) Program is to provide consistent quality 
training, publications, and other materials to prosecutors and public defenders 
throughout California. 
Pursuant to California Penal Code §13836, the LT Program provides trainings for 
district attorneys in the investigation and prosecution of sexual assault cases, 
child sexual exploitation cases, child sex abuse cases, and sexual abuse cases 
involving victims with developmental disabilities. The courses include training in 
the unique emotional trauma experienced by victims of these crimes. 
The LT Program is supported with the following funds: Local Public Prosecutor/Public 
Defender Fund (PPPD), and federal Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence 
Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Components: 0385.161, 0385.541

MEDICAL TRAINING CENTER PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Medical Training Center (EM) Program is to ensure forensic 
examinations are conducted in a standard and consistent manner throughout 
California. This is accomplished through the development of standardized forms, 
protocols, and training for forensic medical examiners, law enforcement, 
prosecutors, and court personnel. 
Pursuant to the Penal Code §13823.93, the Medical Training Center (EM) Program 
funds one hospital-based Subrecipient to provide training for forensic medical 
examiners in cases of sexual assault, child sexual abuse, domestic violence, 
elder/dependent adult abuse, and child physical abuse/neglect. 
The components of the EM Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Maintaining Forensic Medical Evidentiary forms;
• Developing forensic medical reports used for assault against persons with
disabilities;
• Developing and maintaining protocols on how to conduct exams for the
victim populations listed above;
• Developing and maintaining protocols on how to conduct examinations for
assaults against persons with disabilities;
• Providing standardized, advanced, and specialized training for health care
professionals on how to conduct the exams for the victim populations listed
above; and
• Providing technical assistance to sexual assault forensic teams.
The EM Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Medical Training (MTC0) and federal Services*Training* Officers*Prosecutors 
Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Components: 0385.161, 0385.924 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2022-23 MTC0 FY22 $ 125,000 
STOP FFY20 $ 312,650 
STOP FFY22 $ 504,873 
FY 2023-24 MTC0 FY23 $ 125,000 
STOP FFY22 $ 271,489 
STOP FFY23 $ 546,034 
FY 2024-25 Unknown
